Engine: optimize.topology = {enabled, sdBound, maxCuts, maxZips}; metrics note the number of cuts added and zips done.

**Risks:** Only OptCuts' high-level description and CLI parameters were verified. The split/merge candidate filtering, the lambda update rule and the energy normalisation are reconstructed from memory. Darts can create global overlaps (the D-Charts 'gussets'), so run the plan's exact bijectivity check after augmentation and cut off overlapping parts. Changing chart topology invalidates the fixed CSR pattern the plan builds per chart, so rebuild it after each accepted operation. Performance on large charts is uncertain.

**Sources:** Li, Kaufman, Kim, Sorkine-Hornung, Grinspun, 'OptCuts: Joint Optimization of Surface Cuts and Parameterization', SIGGRAPH Asia 2018 - https://www.cs.ubc.ca/labs/imager/tr/2018/OptCuts/ and https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3272127.3275042; OptCuts code/params (bound example 4.1, valid range (4,inf); lambda init 0.999; fixed lambda 0.025) - https://github.com/liminchen/OptCuts; Gu, Gortler, Hoppe 2002, Geometry Images (iterative cut to max-stretch point) - https://hhoppe.com/proj/gim/; Julius et al. 2005, D-Charts 'darts and gussets' - https://www.cs.ubc.ca/~sheffa/papers/EG05.pdf (read)

### F38. Blender Smart UV Project adaptive projection clustering as a fast hard-surface preset and candidate generator

*Impact* medium · *Effort* S · *Feasibility* high · **unverified** · *Target* `src/core/segmentation.js (segmentSmartProject) + engine mode 'smart'; UI preset 'Smart project (Blender-like)'`

**What:** Blender's Smart UV Project picks projection directions adaptively instead of using 6 fixed axes. Faces are sorted by area, largest first. The first direction is the largest face's normal. Untagged faces within half the angle limit (dot > cos(limit/2)) are gathered and averaged, optionally area-weighted, into a projection normal. The next seed is the untagged face whose best dot product against all existing normals is smallest; this repeats while that value < cos(limit), with a default limit of 66°. Every face is then assigned to its best-matching normal and projected orthographically. Islands are the connected components within each group, and they are packed with rotation.

**Why:** The plan's segmentByAxis uses 6 fixed buckets, so 45° bevels and chamfers get up to 1/cos(45°)=1.41x stretch and split arbitrarily. Adaptive directions give bevels their own island. It runs in O(F*K) with K usually 6-40, under 100 ms for 100k faces, and every island is flip-free. It matches what Blender users expect, so it makes a good A/B baseline, and it generates starting candidates for the PartUV-style merge search and for the D-Charts seeds.

**Sketch:** segmentation.js segmentSmartProject(mesh, {angleLimitDeg:66, areaWeight:0, cut}). (1) Precompute face normals n_f and areas A_f; order = faces sorted by A_f descending, ignoring A_f < 1e-12*totalArea (assign these to their neighbour's group later). (2) cosHalf = cos(limit/2), cosFull = cos(limit); tagged = Uint8Array(F); normals = []; pn = n[order[0]]. Loop: gather = all untagged f with dot(n_f,pn) > cosHalf; tag them. avg = sum over gather of w_f*n_f with w_f = areaWeight<=0 ? 1 : areaWeight>=1 ? A_f : A_f*areaWeight + (1-areaWeight). If |avg|>0, push normalize(avg). Then best = argmin over untagged f of max_k dot(n_f, normals[k]). If that min < cosFull: pn = n_best, tag best, continue; else break once normals.length >= 1. Speed-up: maintain maxDot[f] incrementally, updating it only against the newest normal, so the scan is O(F) per iteration. (3) Assign faceGroup[f] = argmax_k dot(n_f, normals[k]). (4) Charts = connected components of faces with equal group that do not cross cut edges, using the plan's connectedComponents with an extra predicate. (5) UVs per chart: orthographic projection onto a basis perpendicular to the group normal. Every face has dot >= cosFull > 0, so there are no flips inside an island. Run the plan's exact self-intersection test, and if an island overlaps itself (folded shapes), split it by depth-sorted connected sub-components. (6) Optional extras beyond Blender: merge small islands (< 3 faces) into the neighbour with the best normal, and run 5 SLIM iterations per island with the boundary free to remove projection stretch. Tests: cube → 6 islands; a cube with 45° chamfers → 18 islands (6 faces + 12 bevels); every face dot with its projection >= cos(66°).

**Risks:** The WebFetch of Blender's source was truncated before the smart-project functions, so the loop structure, the 66° default and the area_weight blend formula are from memory of the Blender 2.9x C++ port. Re-check against the source before claiming parity. Orthographic projection has no shear, but stretch reaches 1/cos(limit) at the group edges (2.46x at 66°), so run a SLIM relax afterwards.

**Sources:** Blender source, uvedit_unwrap_ops.cc (smart_uv_project_calculate_project_normals, smart_project_exec) - https://projects.blender.org/blender/blender/src/branch/main/source/blender/editors/uvedit/uvedit_unwrap_ops.cc (fetch truncated; algorithm from memory of the C++ port); Blender manual, Smart UV Project - https://docs.blender.org/manual/en/latest/modeling/meshes/editing/uv.html#smart-uv-project

### F39. Reflective-symmetry-aware segmentation and seams (mirror labels, symmetric seam editing, optional stacked islands)

*Impact* medium · *Effort* M · *Feasibility* medium · **unverified** · *Target* `new src/core/symmetry.js; segmentation.js wrapper segmentSymmetric; seam-tool.js mirror toggle; pack.js stackMirrored; metrics.js (exclude intentional stacks)`

**What:** Most characters, vehicles and props are bilaterally symmetric. Artists expect mirrored islands and seams, often with a seam along the symmetry plane on the back or underside, and sometimes want mirrored islands stacked to share texels. The approach detects an approximate reflection plane, builds a face-to-face mirror map, segments only one half, mirrors the chart labels and cut flags, and handles charts that cross the plane. A manual seam edit then toggles the mirrored edge as well.

**Why:** Region growing with a global heap and Lloyd reseeding produces asymmetric charts on symmetric input: small tie-breaks send the two sides to different layouts. That looks unprofessional, doubles artist cleanup, and prevents island stacking. Neither v3 nor v4 detects symmetry. Stacking symmetric islands can nearly double effective texel density for mirrored-texture workflows.

**Sketch:** src/core/symmetry.js. (1) Sample M=4000 area-weighted surface points with normals, stored in a uniform grid hash with cell = 2*epsilon, epsilon = 0.004*bboxDiag. (2) Candidate planes: the 3 PCA planes through the area centroid, plus the 3 world-axis planes through the centroid. (3) Score each plane (n,d): reflect points p' = p - 2(p.n - d)n and normals n' = reflect(n_p). Inlier if the nearest sample q satisfies |p'-q| < 2*epsilon and n'.n_q > 0.9. score = inlier fraction. (4) Refine the best plane with ICP-style updates, 8 iterations: for inlier pairs (p,q), n ← normalize(sum over pairs of sign*(p - q)), with sign chosen so the dot with the current n is positive; d ← mean over pairs of ((p+q)/2).n. Accept if score >= 0.9 (UI shows the score; user can force or disable it). (5) Face mirror map: mirror[f] = face containing the reflected centroid, via grid lookup over face centroids with distance < mean edge length and normal check; -1 if unmatched. Side sign s_f = sign(c_f.n - d), with |dist| < 0.5*meanEdge counting as 'centre'. (6) Symmetric segmentation: run any segmenter restricted to faces with s_f <= 0 plus centre faces, then set label[mirror[f]] = label[f] + K for s_f < 0. For a chart that contains centre faces, decide 'plane seam' (default: keep split, so the seam lies on the plane through centre edges) versus 'merged': merge the chart with its mirror if the merged chart passes the distortion check (from the PartUV-style search). Unmatched faces are grown by nearest labels. (7) Cuts: mirrorEdge[e] = edge between mirrored endpoints (vertex mirror by nearest welded vertex within epsilon); the seam tool option 'Mirror' toggles both. (8) Packing: charts with a mirror twin, when opts.stackMirrored is on, get the same packed rectangle with the second's UVs reflected (u → rectW - u); note in metrics that overlap is intentional, and exclude stacked pairs from the bijectivity overlap count. Cost O(F) plus grid lookups; tests: a mirrored torus-knot half, and a symmetric humanoid with jitter 1e-4 giving identical chart counts per side.

**Risks:** Symmetry in real assets is approximate and partial (asymmetric props, a posed arm), so a global plane fails; fall back to asymmetric mode below the score threshold. Partial symmetry detection (Mitra 2006 transform voting) is heavier and was not verified. Stacked islands break lightmap and bake workflows that need unique UVs, so the option must default off.

**Sources:** Mitra, Guibas, Pauly, 'Partial and Approximate Symmetry Detection for 3D Geometry', SIGGRAPH 2006 - https://doi.org/10.1145/1141911.1141924 (from memory); Julius et al. 2005, D-Charts, conclusions (symmetric charts identified as desirable future work) - https://www.cs.ubc.ca/~sheffa/papers/EG05.pdf (read)

### F40. Artist-seam benchmark and seam-quality metrics (what can be borrowed from learned seam work like SeamGPT/ArtUV)

*Impact* medium · *Effort* S · *Feasibility* high · **unverified** · *Target* `src/core/metrics.js (seamMatch, seamStraightness, partuvAreaDistortion); engine.js benchmark ('Artist' candidate); src/ui/charts.js (Pareto view); tests/fixtures`

**What:** Learned seam generators (SeamGPT, ArtUV, and PartField inside PartUV) need large transformer or feature networks with GPU inference and cannot ship in a no-build, file:// JS toolkit. Their most transferable contribution is the evaluation protocol: artist seams recovered from existing UV island boundaries serve as ground truth, and automatic seams are judged on semantic fidelity (chart count, seam length) as well as distortion. This finding adds that protocol as a user-facing benchmark. Imported UVs become a first-class candidate ('Artist'), and length-weighted seam precision/recall against them are reported.

**Why:** The plan's benchmark scores stretch, coverage and bijectivity, all geometric. None of those captures whether seams are where a human would put them, which is the main complaint about xatlas and Blender output (PartUV: 975-1360 charts on average vs about 49). An artist-seam F-score, visible seam length and chart count, set against imported artist UVs, makes the A/B compare meaningful. It is also the signal needed to tune every constant in the other findings.

**Sketch:** metrics.js additions. (1) artistSeams(mesh, attrs) = edges with the UV_SEAM bit (from the feature-seeding finding), plus mesh boundary edges excluded from scoring. (2) Tolerant matching: multi-source Dijkstra over mesh edges from the vertices of G (artist seams), giving distG[v]; edge e counts as matched if max(distG[a], distG[b]) <= delta, with delta = 2*meanEdgeLength (option). The same from S (auto seams) gives distS. precision = sum over S edges with distG <= delta of len / sum over S of len; recall = sum over G edges with distS <= delta of len / sum over G of len; F1 = 2PR/(P+R). (3) Also report chartCount ratio (auto/artist), seamLength ratio, visibleSeamLength (visibility finding), seam straightness = sum of turning angles along seam chains per unit length (lower is cleaner), plus PartUV's area metric max over charts of mean of max(s,1/s) for cross-paper comparison. (4) Engine: when the imported mesh has UVs, the benchmark evaluates the 'Artist' UV as a candidate with computeMetrics, reusing faceChart from the UV connectivity. The benchmark table ranks presets by a Pareto view of distortion vs seam F1 vs chart count. (5) Test fixtures: a handful of CC0 assets with artist UVs, inlined as JS strings for node tests (no fetch), each with expected metric ranges. Learned inference is explicitly out of scope: ONNX Runtime Web WASM plus model weights would be tens of MB inlined as base64 and still need a pure-JS fallback.

**Risks:** Artist UVs often contain overlaps, stacked islands and UDIMs, so treat their bijectivity metrics as informational. Seam-F1 rewards imitation, not optimality; show it next to distortion, not as a single score. SeamGPT and ArtUV were checked only at abstract/search-snippet level; their exact benchmark metrics were not read.

**Sources:** Li et al., 'Auto-Regressive Surface Cutting' (SeamGPT), arXiv 2025 - https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.18017 (abstract-level: seams as next-token sequences of quantised 3D segments; trained on 560K Objaverse/Objaverse-XL/3D-FUTURE meshes with artist seams from UV island boundaries); ArtUV: Artist-style UV Unwrapping, arXiv 2025 - https://arxiv.org/pdf/2509.20710 (not read); PartUV 2025 metrics (chart count, seam length, angular/area distortion) - https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.16659

## Browser/web-platform techniques to make the pure-JS UV kernel fast and robust without a build step (Blob workers on file://, transfer/SAB, worker pools, WASM SIMD, WebGPU/WebGL validation, OffscreenCanvas, V8 hot loops, IndexedDB, File System Access, PWA on GitHub Pages, Three.js r141 constraints)

### F41. Hardened Blob-URL engine bootstrap: capability handshake, zero-copy transfer helper, hot-standby cancel, live SLIM preview, time-sliced main-thread fallback

*Impact* high · *Effort* M · *Feasibility* high · **unverified** · *Target* `src/client/engine-client.js, src/core/worker-main.js, src/core/engine.js (generator form of unwrap/relax/optimizeSearch)`

**What:** A set of concrete hardening steps around the planned `new Worker(URL.createObjectURL(new Blob([UVCore.source()...])))`: (1) the worker reports a capability object on 'ready' so all feature gating (SAB, SIMD, OffscreenCanvas, WebGPU, CompressionStream) happens in one place; (2) a generic transfer-list builder so every typed array crosses the boundary zero-copy; (3) progress throttling; (4) a pre-warmed standby worker so Cancel is instant instead of terminate+respawn+re-setMesh; (5) streamed UV previews so users watch SLIM converge; (6) if the worker cannot be created (Safari file:// behaviour is unverified), the main-thread fallback runs the same engine as a generator in 12 ms slices so the UI never freezes.

**Why:** v3 blocks the main thread for the whole unwrap. The v4 plan falls back only after a 3 s timeout, then runs synchronously on the main thread (UI freezes on exactly the browsers where workers fail), and cancel = terminate + respawn + resend mesh (compile of the kernel source + buildMesh; for 100k faces this is roughly 100-300 ms before the user can act again, estimate). The standby worker makes cancel effectively 0 ms; onerror/SecurityError fallback is immediate instead of 3 s; the time-sliced fallback keeps 60 fps responsiveness; live preview is a new user-facing feature (optimizer animation) at ~2.4 MB/100 ms for 100k faces.

**Sketch:** Source: `const src = UVCore.source() + '\nworkerMain(UVCore.build());\n//# sourceURL=uvcore-worker.js'` (sourceURL gives readable DevTools stack traces). Create one Blob URL and keep it for the page lifetime (reused by the pool and respawns; revoke only on pagehide, since revoking before the worker script is loaded is unreliable across engines). Wrap `new Worker(url)` in try/catch (SecurityError -> fallback immediately), attach `worker.onerror` and `onmessageerror` -> fallback immediately; keep the ready timeout but raise it to ~8 s (slow mobile compile) because real failures surface via onerror. Handshake from worker-main: `postMessage({type:'ready', caps:{secure:self.isSecureContext, coi:self.crossOriginIsolated===true, sab:typeof SharedArrayBuffer==='function'&&self.crossOriginIsolated===true, wasm:typeof WebAssembly==='object', simd:C.wasmSimdSupported(), offscreen2d:typeof OffscreenCanvas==='function', gpu:!!(self.navigator&&self.navigator.gpu), cores:(self.navigator&&navigator.hardwareConcurrency)||2, gzip:typeof CompressionStream==='function'}})`; EngineClient exposes `.caps`. Transfer helper (in worker-main and engine-client): `function collectTransfer(v, set, depth){ if(depth>4||v==null) return set; if(ArrayBuffer.isView(v)){ const b=v.buffer; if(!(typeof SharedArrayBuffer==='function'&&b instanceof SharedArrayBuffer) && v.byteOffset===0 && v.byteLength===b.byteLength) set.add(b); return set;} if(typeof v==='object') for(const k in v) collectTransfer(v[k], set, depth+1); return set; }` then `postMessage(msg, [...collectTransfer(msg, new Set(), 0)])` (Set dedups: listing the same buffer twice throws DataCloneError). Contract rule: core results must allocate exact-size arrays (no subarray views) or they will be cloned; sender must never touch a transferred array (it is detached, length 0). The client keeps canonical `positions` and `manualCut` and sends `positions.slice()` so respawn/standby needs no reload. Progress throttle in worker: `let last=0; const progress=(s,d,t)=>{const now=performance.now(); if(d===t||now-last>=50){last=now; postMessage({id,type:'progress',stage:s,done:d,total:t});}}`. Live preview: add `previewMs` (default 120) to UnwrapOpts; inside optimize after each outer iteration, if elapsed >= previewMs, write current per-chart UV into a fresh Float32Array(6F) using each chart's last packing transform (or per-chart normalised layout on first run) and `postMessage({id,type:'preview',uv},[uv.buffer])`; UI keeps only the newest pending preview and applies it in requestAnimationFrame via `attr.array.set(uv); attr.needsUpdate=true`. Hot standby: after the first successful unwrap spawn worker B and replay setMesh(positions.slice()) + setCut(manualCut.slice()); `cancel()` = terminate A, reject pending promises with {cancelled:true}, promote B, spawn a new standby in the background (skip standby above ~300k faces to cap memory). Time-sliced fallback: refactor `UVEngine.unwrap` into `*unwrapSteps(opts)` that yields after each chart flatten, each SLIM iteration and each pack trial; sync `unwrap()` just drains the generator (node tests unchanged). Main-thread runner: `function runSliced(gen){return new Promise((res,rej)=>{const ch=new MessageChannel(); ch.port1.onmessage=step; function step(){const t0=performance.now(); try{let r; while(!(r=gen.next()).done){ if(performance.now()-t0>12){ ch.port2.postMessage(0); return; } } res(r.value);}catch(e){rej(e);} } step();});}` (MessageChannel avoids the 4 ms setTimeout clamp); cancel in fallback = `gen.return()`.

**Risks:** Safari behaviour for blob: workers created from a file:// page could not be verified (search results only cover sandboxed-origin cases) - the immediate-fallback path must be tested manually on Safari. Standby worker doubles kernel memory. Generators add negligible overhead only if yields are coarse (per chart / per iteration, never per triangle). Preview messages must be dropped, not queued, or they back up the main thread.

**Sources:** docs/ARCHITECTURE.md section 4.12 (local contract: Blob worker, 3 s ready timeout, terminate+respawn cancel); MDN Secure Contexts (file: URLs are potentially trustworthy; dedicated workers are secure when their owner is) - https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/Security/Secure_Contexts; w3c/webappsec-mixed-content issue 41 (browser differences for blob: workers/iframes, Safari refusal in sandboxed case) - https://github.com/w3c/webappsec-mixed-content/issues/41; Chromium Intent to Ship: opaque origin for data: URL workers - https://groups.google.com/a/chromium.org/g/blink-dev/c/4Hy56DZjF34; MDN Transferable objects - https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/API/Web_Workers_API/Transferable_objects

### F42. Worker pool: parallel per-chart flatten+optimize, parallel optimizeSearch trials, parallel pack scale bracketing (MessageChannel wiring, LPT scheduling)

*Impact* high · *Effort* L · *Feasibility* medium · **unverified** · *Target* `new src/client/worker-pool.js; src/core/engine.js (stage split + chartJob), src/core/worker-main.js (ops attachHelper, helperPort, chartJob, trial, packTrial)`

**What:** Use N-1 extra Blob-URL workers running the identical kernel. The engine worker does serial stages (segmentation, cutToDisk, packing, metrics) and farms out embarrassingly parallel work: per-chart initChart+SLIM/ARAP jobs, whole optimizeSearch trials, and candidate packing scales. Workers are wired directly to the engine worker with MessagePorts, so no nested workers and no main-thread hop.

**Why:** The v4 plan is single-worker, so per-chart optimization (typically the largest stage) runs serially. With 4 helpers and flatten+optimize at ~60-70% of total time, Amdahl gives ~1.8-2.1x end-to-end for unwrap (estimate); optimizeSearch's 3 independent trials give ~2.8x, or the same wall time can evaluate a larger grid (e.g. 5 angles x {LSCM, Tutte}) for better quality. Parallel scale bracketing replaces ~6-8 serial pack attempts with 3 rounds of parallel attempts.

**Sketch:** Pool size P = clamp((navigator.hardwareConcurrency||4) - 1, 1, 6); spawn lazily only when faceCount > ~3000 (below that, spawn/compile cost ~tens of ms each outweighs gains) and keep them warm. Wiring: main thread creates `const ch = new MessageChannel()` per helper, sends `engineWorker.postMessage({op:'attachHelper', port: ch.port1}, [ch.port1])` and `helper.postMessage({op:'helperPort', port: ch.port2}, [ch.port2])`; avoids nested `new Worker(blob:)` inside a worker, which is fragile on opaque file:// origins. Engine refactor (keeps the sync API for node/main-thread): split unwrap into `segmentStage(opts) -> charts[]`, `chartJob(chartLocal, opts) -> {uv: Float64Array(2n), stats}` (pure: C.initChart + C.optimize on local lp/tris), `assembleStage(results) -> pack + metrics`. In the worker, `unwrapParallel` awaits the jobs. Job payload `{jobId, lp: Float64Array(3n), tris: Int32Array(3T), pins?, opts}` built with exact-size arrays so they transfer; results transferred back. Scheduling: cost estimate w = T^1.25 (SLIM iteration ~ O(T x PCG iterations), PCG iterations ~ sqrt(n)); sort jobs by w descending (LPT rule: 4/3-approximation for makespan); dynamic pull queue - each executor (helpers plus the engine worker itself) takes the next job on completion. Batch small charts: concatenate consecutive jobs into one packet until sum(T) >= 2000 or 64 charts, so per-message overhead (~0.1-0.3 ms clone/dispatch, estimate) stays below ~5% of work. Determinism: never share RNG state; per-chart seed = mulberry32(hash(globalSeed, chartId)); assemble by chartId so output is bit-identical to the serial path (add a node test comparing serial vs simulated-parallel order). optimizeSearch: send each trial (`{op:'trial', positions copy, cut copy, opts}`) to a different helper that has the mesh (setMesh once per helper, cached by mesh hash) and pick the best with metrics.compareMetrics. Packing: round r evaluates K = P scales s_k = s_lo * (s_hi/s_lo)^(k/(K+1)), each helper packs all chart rasters at s_k and returns {fits, coverage}; new bracket = [largest fitting, smallest failing]; 3 rounds with K=4 shrink the ratio by 5^3 = 125x; final placement recomputed once in the engine worker. Cancel: terminate all pool workers, respawn lazily. Single huge chart: no speedup without SharedArrayBuffer (shared-memory SpMV is memory-bandwidth bound, ~1.5x at best) - explicitly out of scope; report 'parallel: n charts on P workers' in timings.

**Risks:** Memory scales with P (each worker has its own heap + kernel code). hardwareConcurrency is spoofed/low under Firefox resistFingerprinting and capped on some platforms; mobile thermal throttling makes >2 workers counterproductive - expose a 'Threads' setting (auto/1/2/4/6). Gains depend on stage breakdown; measure with UnwrapResult.timings before committing. Error in one job must reject the whole unwrap and fall back to serial.

**Sources:** docs/ARCHITECTURE.md 4.11 (optimizeSearch tries 3 angles; packing secant scale search; charts[i].local independent per chart); MDN MessageChannel / MessagePort transferable - https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/API/MessageChannel; xatlas (per-chart parameterization is parallelised with a task scheduler in the C++ original) - https://github.com/jpcy/xatlas

### F43. Single sw.js for GitHub Pages: offline PWA cache + opt-in cross-origin isolation (SharedArrayBuffer, Atomics cancel, fine timers) with strict fallback

*Impact* medium · *Effort* M · *Feasibility* high · verified · *Target* `new sw.js and manifest.webmanifest at repo root; src/ui/main.js (registration, update toast, launchQueue); src/client/engine-client.js + worker-pool.js (SAB gating)`

**What:** GitHub Pages cannot send COOP/COEP headers, so SharedArrayBuffer is normally unavailable. A service worker served from the same origin can re-emit every same-origin response with those headers (the coi-serviceworker technique), which makes the page crossOriginIsolated after one reload. The same service worker also precaches the app for offline use and enables manifest file_handlers. On file:// nothing is registered and everything uses the transfer path.

**Why:** The v4 plan has no offline mode and no shared memory. This adds: installable offline app on GitHub Pages; Atomics-based cooperative cancel that keeps worker state (no respawn); zero-copy mesh sharing across pool workers (no N copies of positions/adjacency); fine-grained performance.now() for the benchmark panel (timers are coarsened when not isolated); OS 'Open with' on Chromium via launchQueue. All strictly optional, gated on `crossOriginIsolated === true`.

**Sketch:** Register only when `'serviceWorker' in navigator && (location.protocol === 'https:' || location.hostname === 'localhost')` via `navigator.serviceWorker.register('./sw.js')` (relative path so the scope is /<repo>/ on Pages). Do NOT also ship coi-serviceworker.js: two service workers cannot control the same scope, so fold its logic into sw.js. sw.js: `const VERSION='uvtk-4.0.0'; const ASSETS=['./','index.html','src/ui/styles.css', ...every src/*.js..., 'vendor/three/three.min.js', 'vendor/three/GLTFLoader.js', ..., 'vendor/chart.min.js'];` install: `e.waitUntil(caches.open(VERSION).then(c=>c.addAll(ASSETS)))`; activate: delete caches whose key !== VERSION, then `clients.claim()`. fetch (same-origin GET only): vendor/ cache-first; index.html and src/ stale-while-revalidate (`caches.match(req,{ignoreSearch:true})`, fetch in background and put). Header injection when isolation is enabled (flag stored via postMessage from the page into a module-level variable, default on): `function isolate(r){ if(r.status===0) return r; const h=new Headers(r.headers); h.set('Cross-Origin-Opener-Policy','same-origin'); h.set('Cross-Origin-Embedder-Policy','require-corp'); return new Response(r.body,{status:r.status,statusText:r.statusText,headers:h}); }` applied to responses served to the page. Use require-corp rather than credentialless because Safari lacks credentialless and every asset is vendored same-origin (v3's CDN three.js must stay removed). First visit: after `navigator.serviceWorker.ready`, if `!self.crossOriginIsolated && !sessionStorage.getItem('uvtk-coi-reload')` then set the flag and `location.reload()` once (loop guard). Update UX: if `registration.waiting` show 'Update available', on click post 'skipWaiting' and reload on `controllerchange`. SAB paths (only if crossOriginIsolated): (a) `const cancelFlag = new Int32Array(new SharedArrayBuffer(4))` sent to workers; `shouldCancel = () => Atomics.load(cancelFlag,0) !== 0`; client cancel does `Atomics.store(cancelFlag,0,1)` and awaits a {cancelled:true} result within ~500 ms before falling back to terminate; (b) optional allocator hook in the kernel `C.alloc(Ctor, n)` that returns SAB-backed arrays for read-only mesh data (positions, weldPos, adjacency CSR) so pool workers share them; (c) a shared Float64Array(4) progress block polled in rAF instead of progress messages. manifest.webmanifest: name, start_url './', display 'standalone', 192/512 PNG icons, and `file_handlers: [{action:'./', accept:{'model/obj':['.obj'],'model/gltf-binary':['.glb'],'model/stl':['.stl']}}]`; page: `if('launchQueue' in window) launchQueue.setConsumer(p => p.files.forEach(openHandle))`. Also call `navigator.storage.persist()` when served.

**Risks:** COEP require-corp blocks any cross-origin subresource (CDN scripts, remote fonts, embedded iframes, external images) - keep everything vendored. One forced reload on first visit. Stale-cache bugs if VERSION is not bumped on release (add it to the release checklist / CHANGELOG). Not available from file://. SAB code paths are a second implementation to test; keep them thin.

**Sources:** coi-serviceworker README (reload on first load, sets COOP/COEP, credentialless by default with require-corp degrade, not usable from file:// or a CDN) - https://github.com/gzuidhof/coi-serviceworker; MDN Secure Contexts (service workers require secure contexts) - https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/Security/Secure_Contexts; MDN crossOriginIsolated / SharedArrayBuffer security requirements - https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/JavaScript/Reference/Global_Objects/SharedArrayBuffer#security_requirements

### F44. V8 hot-loop hygiene: fixed-pattern CSR/BSR assembly replacing the Map-based TripletBuilder, trig-free allocation-free svd2, monomorphic Float64 kernel

*Impact* high · *Effort* M · *Feasibility* high · **unverified** · *Target* `src/core/solvers.js (SparsePattern, BSR, envelope LDLT), src/core/math.js (svd2Into, polar2Into), src/core/optimize.js, src/core/mesh.js (Float64 positions), tests/bench.js`

**What:** Concrete JS-engine performance rules applied to the existing kernel. (a) Split sparse assembly into a one-time symbolic phase and an O(T) numeric phase that writes into preallocated Float64Arrays through a per-triangle slot table. (b) Use 2x2 block sparse rows (BSR) for the coupled SLIM system. (c) Replace the trig-based svd2 with a sqrt-only version that writes into an output array. (d) Keep every kernel hot function monomorphic on Float64Array. (e) Hoist `C.x` lookups into factory-local constants.

**Why:** The planned SLIM/ARAP rebuilds the system every iteration through TripletBuilder: Map lookups plus boxing (a Map stores tagged values, so every non-integer double set allocates a HeapNumber), per-row Array.from + sort, and GC pressure. Fixed-pattern assembly is typically 5-20x faster for assembly (estimate), and BSR SpMV is typically 1.3-2x faster than scalar CSR on 2D-coupled systems because it loads half as many column indices. The trig-free svd2 is roughly 3-6x faster per call (estimate - benchmark it). Together these likely matter more than any WASM or GPU work, and they need no new platform features.

**Sketch:** (1) Symbolic phase, once per chart: collect undirected vertex pairs from tris, pack key = min*n + max into a Float64Array (exact below 2^53), native `keys.sort()` (TypedArray sort without a comparator is numeric), unique; degree count -> rowPtr (include diagonal); fill colIdx and sort each row with `colIdx.subarray(a,b).sort()` (one-time, allocation acceptable). Slot table `slot = new Int32Array(9*T)`: slot[9t+3a+b] = position of vertex tris[3t+b] in row tris[3t+a], found by binary search in colIdx (O(log deg)). Numeric phase, every iteration: `vals.fill(0); for (t) for (a) for (b) vals[slot[9*t+3*a+b]] += w_t * K_ab;` with zero allocation. SLIM (coupled u,v): BSR with the same pattern, `vals = new Float64Array(4*nnzB)` stored row-major [a b; c d] per block, block slot = slot*4. BSR SpMV: `for(let i=0;i<nb;i++){ let y0=0,y1=0; for(let p=rowPtr[i],e=rowPtr[i+1];p<e;p++){ const j=colIdx[p], x0=x[2*j], x1=x[2*j+1], q=4*p; y0+=v[q]*x0+v[q+1]*x1; y1+=v[q+2]*x0+v[q+3]*x1; } y[2*i]=y0; y[2*i+1]=y1; }`. The block-Jacobi (block2) preconditioner reads the diagonal blocks directly. ARAP/Tutte/LSCM with fixed pins have a constant matrix: optionally factor once with RCM ordering + envelope (profile) LDL^T when the estimated profile is <= ~8e6 entries (64 MB), and use PCG warm-started from the previous iterate otherwise. (2) Trig-free svd2Into(a,b,c,d,out /*Float64Array(6)*/): E=(a+d)/2, F=(a-d)/2, G=(c+b)/2, H=(c-b)/2, Q=sqrt(E*E+H*H), R=sqrt(F*F+G*G); (cA1,sA1) = R>eps ? (F/R, G/R) : (1,0); (cA2,sA2) = Q>eps ? (E/Q, H/Q) : (1,0); cs = cA1*cA2 - sA1*sA2 and sn = sA1*cA2 + cA1*sA2 (cos and sin of a1+a2); cosP = sqrt(max(0,(1+cs)/2)); sinP = (sn>=0?1:-1)*sqrt(max(0,(1-cs)/2)); theta = a1 - phi: cosT = cA1*cosP + sA1*sinP, sinT = sA1*cosP - cA1*sinP; out = [Q+R, Q-R, cosP, sinP, cosT, sinT]. The principal half-angle can only shift phi and theta together by pi, which leaves U S V^T unchanged. Test by reconstructing J to 1e-12 against the existing svd2 on 1e5 random matrices, including det<0 and near-degenerate cases. polar2Into likewise. (3) Monomorphism: in buildMesh convert positions once (`const P = Float64Array.from(positions)`) and let every kernel function take Float64Array only; convert to Float32Array only at the UI/GPU boundary. Mixing Float32Array and Float64Array at one call site makes the element-access inline cache polymorphic. (4) At the top of each factory: `const svd2Into = C.svd2Into, pcg = C.pcg;`. C is built by computed-key assignment of many exports and may not have fast properties, and a local const also lets the JIT inline. (5) Misc: `Math.sqrt(x*x+y*y)` instead of Math.hypot (historically much slower in V8); no subarray, destructuring or closure creation inside per-element loops; a per-chart workspace object holding all scratch Float64Arrays, reused across iterations; clamp |w| < 1e-290 to 0 in weights, since subnormal doubles cause large x86 slowdowns; argsort by float key via `idx.sort((i,j)=>key[i]-key[j])` on an Int32Array, or pack (quantized31bitKey*2^21 + index) into a Float64Array and call native sort when n < 2^21. (6) Measurement: tests/bench.js run with `node --cpu-prof` and `--trace-deopt`, and stage timings kept in UnwrapResult.timings.

**Risks:** Speedup figures are estimates from general V8 behaviour, not measured on this code - benchmark before and after. The envelope Cholesky memory must be guarded by a profile estimate. Changing svd2's signature touches existing tests; keep the old function as a wrapper. Dictionary-mode for C is plausible but unconfirmed; hoisting is harmless either way.

**Sources:** src/core/solvers.js (TripletBuilder uses one Map per row; csrFromTriplets sorts Array.from(keys)) and src/core/math.js (svd2 uses 2x atan2 + 4 trig calls and returns an object) - local code; V8 blog: Elements kinds in V8 (2017) - https://v8.dev/blog/elements-kinds; V8 blog: Fast properties in V8 (2017) - https://v8.dev/blog/fast-properties; libigl SLIM / ARAP precompute-then-solve structure - https://github.com/libigl/libigl

### F45. Runtime-assembled WebAssembly SIMD micro-kernels (bytes emitted by JS, no base64, no toolchain) for BSR SpMV / dot / axpy with JS fallback

*Impact* medium · *Effort* L · *Feasibility* medium · verified · *Target* `new src/core/wasm.js (assembler + kernels, defined before solvers or injected into solvers via C.wasmKernels); src/core/solvers.js pcg selects backend; tests/wasm.test.js`

**What:** A ~150-200 line assembler inside a UVCore factory that builds a tiny wasm module as a Uint8Array at startup: header, type, import (memory), function, export and code sections, with LEB128 helpers. It emits SIMD (f64x2) versions of PCG's hot loops, checks support with WebAssembly.validate, and falls back to a scalar-wasm build or plain JS. There is no binary blob in the repo and no wat2wasm step, so the kernel stays readable, reviewable and serializable into the Blob worker.

**Why:** Neither v3 nor the v4 plan uses WASM. A base64 blob would need an external toolchain, which conflicts with 'no build step'. Runtime assembly keeps the constraint. Expected gain is modest: dense f64x2 ops (dot, axpy) roughly 2x, BSR SpMV roughly 1.2-1.5x (memory-bound, indices cannot be gathered cheaply), so about 1.3-1.8x on PCG overall (estimates). Do this only after the pure-JS BSR/fixed-pattern work.

**Sketch:** Encoding helpers: `uleb(n)` (7-bit groups, 0x80 continuation), `sleb(n)`, `section(id, bytes) = [id, ...uleb(bytes.length), ...bytes]`, `vec(items) = [...uleb(items.length), ...items.flat()]`, `name(s)` = uleb(len) + UTF-8. Module: magic `0x00 0x61 0x73 0x6D`, version `0x01 0 0 0`. Type section (1): functypes `0x60 params results` with valtypes i32=0x7F, f64=0x7C, v128=0x7B. Import section (2): module 'env', field 'mem', kind 0x02 (memory), limits `0x00 minPages` (JS passes `new WebAssembly.Memory({initial})`). Function section (3), export section (7, kind 0x00 = func), code section (10): each body is `uleb(size)`, then local declarations `vec([count, type])`, then instructions, then `0x0B`. Scalar opcodes (spec): local.get 0x20, local.set 0x21, local.tee 0x22, i32.load 0x28 (memarg align=2, offset=0), f64.load 0x2B (align=3), f64.store 0x39, i32.const 0x41, i32.add 0x6A, i32.shl 0x74, i32.lt_u 0x49, f64.add 0xA0, f64.mul 0xA2, block 0x02 / loop 0x03 (blocktype 0x40), br 0x0C, br_if 0x0D, end 0x0B. SIMD opcodes (verified in BinarySIMD.md; prefix 0xFD followed by the varuint32 opcode, so values >= 0x80 take two bytes): v128.load 0x00 (memarg align=3), v128.store 0x0B, v128.const 0x0C, f64x2.splat 0x14, f64x2.extract_lane 0x21 (+ lane byte), f64x2.replace_lane 0x22, f64x2.abs 0xEC, f64x2.neg 0xED, f64x2.sqrt 0xEF, f64x2.add 0xF0, f64x2.sub 0xF1, f64x2.mul 0xF2, f64x2.div 0xF3, f64x2.min 0xF4, f64x2.max 0xF5 (e.g. f64x2.add encodes as FD F0 01). Kernels: dot(x,y,n): accumulate `f64x2.add(acc, f64x2.mul(v128.load(x+i), v128.load(y+i)))` in steps of 16 bytes, scalar tail, return lane0+lane1. axpy(a,x,y,n): `v128.store(y+i, f64x2.add(v128.load(y+i), f64x2.mul(f64x2.splat(a), v128.load(x+i))))`. xpby for the PCG direction update likewise. bsrSpmv(rowPtr,col,val,x,y,nb): store BSR blocks column-major [a c b d] so `v128.load(val+32p)` = column0 and `v128.load(val+32p+16)` = column1; for each block, `x0=f64.load(x+16j)`, `x1=f64.load(x+16j+8)`, `acc = acc + col0*splat(x0) + col1*splat(x1)`; store acc at y+16i. Memory layout per chart solve (one arena, all offsets multiples of 8): rowPtr Int32(nb+1) | pad | colIdx Int32(nnzB) | pad | vals F64(4 nnzB) | x, b, r, z, p, Ap F64(2 nb each). Copy the matrix in once per solve and run all PCG iterations in place. After any `memory.grow()` re-create every JS view, because grow detaches the old ArrayBuffer. Instantiate with `await WebAssembly.instantiate(bytes, {env:{mem}})` inside the worker (async compile; the main-thread fallback should also use the async API, since synchronous compile of large modules on the main thread is restricted in Chromium). Feature gate: `WebAssembly.validate(simdBytes)`; if false, emit the scalar variant from the same assembler; if WebAssembly is missing, use JS. Node tests: compare wasm vs JS results on random SPD BSR systems to 1e-12 relative, and assert that the module validates. Baseline SIMD shipped roughly in Chrome 91 / Firefox 89 / Safari 16.4 (versions not re-verified here); runtime validate makes the exact versions irrelevant.

**Risks:** Hand-assembling is error-prone; validate() only returns a boolean, so compile with `new WebAssembly.Module(bytes)` in a try/catch in tests to get the error message. Gains may be small versus well-JITted JS; ship behind a 'Kernel: auto/js/wasm' debug setting and keep it only if the benchmark shows >= 1.3x. The copy into wasm memory costs O(nnz) per solve (negligible compared with iterations).

**Sources:** WebAssembly SIMD proposal binary encoding table (0xFD prefix, varuint32 opcodes) - https://github.com/WebAssembly/simd/blob/main/proposals/simd/BinarySIMD.md; WebAssembly Core spec, binary instructions - https://webassembly.github.io/spec/core/binary/instructions.html

### F46. GPU-rasterised texel-space UV validator (overlap/fold texels, coverage, padding gap, mip-safe level) via WebGL2 render targets in Three.js r141 - and a clear 'no WebGPU in the kernel' decision

*Impact* high · *Effort* M · *Feasibility* high · **unverified** · *Target* `new src/ui/uv-validate.js (WebGL passes + overlay texture); EDT/gap computation as pure function in src/core/metrics.js (rasterMetrics(labelMap, countMap, S)); uv-view.js overlay; exporters report fields`

**What:** Render all packed UV triangles into an offscreen render target with additive blending. GPU fill rules guarantee that triangles sharing an edge never both cover a pixel centre, so any texel with count >= 2 is a real overlap: two charts overlapping, or a fold or flip inside a chart. A second pass renders chart IDs; a CPU distance transform on that ID map then gives the minimum gap between charts in texels, padding violations and a 'mip-safe level'. The results appear as an overlay heatmap in the UV view and as report metrics. For WebGPU, the recommendation is to keep it out of the solver: the per-triangle work is too cheap and readback latency too high.

**Why:** v3 has no overlap validation. The v4 plan's exact bijectivity check is geometric and resolution-blind. This validator is resolution-aware, which is what matters for baking: it catches overlaps smaller than a triangle, measures real texel padding and mip bleeding (which geometric tests do not report), and runs in a few ms for 100k triangles at 4096^2 on the existing Three.js renderer, with no new dependency. It also serves as a cross-check for the exact CPU test and the packer (property test: packer output must give overlapTexels == 0 and minGap >= paddingTexels).

**Sketch:** Pass A (count): S = min(packing.resolution, renderer.capabilities.maxTextureSize, 4096). `const rt = new THREE.WebGLRenderTarget(S, S, {type: THREE.UnsignedByteType, format: THREE.RGBAFormat, depthBuffer: false, stencilBuffer: false})`. Non-indexed BufferGeometry whose 'position' is (2u-1, 2v-1, 0) per corner, taken directly from the packed per-corner UVs; shared chart vertices are bit-identical floats, so edges match exactly. `new THREE.RawShaderMaterial({vertexShader: 'precision highp float; invariant gl_Position; attribute vec3 position; void main(){ gl_Position = vec4(position.xy, 0.0, 1.0); }', fragmentShader: 'precision mediump float; void main(){ gl_FragColor = vec4(1.0/255.0, 0.0, 0.0, 1.0); }', blending: THREE.CustomBlending, blendEquation: THREE.AddEquation, blendSrc: THREE.OneFactor, blendDst: THREE.OneFactor, side: THREE.DoubleSide, depthTest: false, depthWrite: false})`. DoubleSide is required because flipped triangles are back-facing; RawShaderMaterial avoids tone mapping and encoding chunks. Render with an OrthographicCamera(-1,1,1,-1,0,1) after `renderer.setRenderTarget(rt); renderer.setClearColor(0x000000, 0); renderer.clear()`, then restore the previous target. Read back in strips: `renderer.readRenderTargetPixels(rt, 0, y, S, 512, buf)` into a reused Uint8Array(S*512*4). Stats: covered = #(R>=1), overlapTexels = #(R>=2), coverage = covered/S^2, overlapRatio = overlapTexels/covered; build a Uint8 mask for the overlay. Pass B (IDs): NoBlending, colour = chartId+1 encoded in RGB (r = id & 255, g = (id>>8) & 255, b = (id>>16) & 255) via a per-vertex attribute; read back into an Int32Array label map L (0 = empty). Gap: run an exact 2D EDT (Felzenszwalb-Huttenlocher 1D pass on rows, then columns), propagating the nearest seed index so every texel gets (d, label). For each 4-neighbour pair with different non-zero labels la != lb, gap = d_a + d_b + 1 (in texels); keep minGap per chart and overall. Report paddingViolation = minGap < paddingTexels, and mipSafeLevel = max(0, floor(log2(minGap)) - 1). The latter is a heuristic: at mip k a texel spans 2^k base texels and bilinear filtering reaches one neighbour, so the gap needs to be about 2^(k+1). CPU fallback (no WebGL2, or in the worker): a JS scanline rasteriser with the same top-left rule into a Uint8Array at 1024^2, O(covered texels). Degenerate slivers thinner than a texel can be missed; label the metric 'at S x S'. WebGPU decision: SLIM's local step (2x2 SVD per triangle) costs about 10-30 ns in JS, so ~1-3 ms per iteration at 100k triangles, which is below one writeBuffer + dispatch + mapAsync round trip; PCG needs dot-product readbacks every iteration, so it is latency-bound. Do not use WebGPU in the kernel. An optional later step: port passes A/B to WebGPU compute in a worker for 8K validation (navigator.gpu is available in Chrome 113+, Firefox 141+ on Windows, Safari 26), with inline WGSL strings and this WebGL2 path as the fallback.

**Risks:** Readback memory at 4096^2 RGBA is 64 MB, so read in strips. Some mobile GPUs cap textures at 4096. The fill-rule guarantee assumes no MSAA and identical vertex inputs (use invariant gl_Position). The validator is sampling-based (texel centres), so it complements rather than replaces the exact test. The r141 RawShaderMaterial and readRenderTargetPixels details come from general knowledge, not re-verified here.

**Sources:** GLSL Programming/Rasterization (OpenGL rule: a fragment centre on a shared edge is produced by exactly one polygon) - https://en.wikibooks.org/wiki/GLSL_Programming/Rasterization; F. Giesen, A trip through the Graphics Pipeline 2011 part 6 (top-left fill rule; adjacent triangles never double-cover) - https://fgiesen.wordpress.com/2011/07/06/a-trip-through-the-graphics-pipeline-2011-part-6/; web.dev: WebGPU supported in major browsers - https://web.dev/blog/webgpu-supported-major-browsers; gpuweb Implementation Status - https://github.com/gpuweb/gpuweb/wiki/Implementation-Status; Felzenszwalb & Huttenlocher, Distance Transforms of Sampled Functions (2012) - https://theoryofcomputing.org/articles/v008a019/

### F47. OffscreenCanvas UV layout / heatmap / preview rendering inside a worker with shared drawing code and main-thread fallback

*Impact* medium · *Effort* M · *Feasibility* high · verified · *Target* `new src/render/uvraster.js (DOM-free drawing, serializable), src/core/worker-main.js op renderUV, src/io/exporters.js exportUVPNG delegates to engine client, src/ui/uv-view.js bitmap preview`

**What:** Move UV PNG export (wire / chart-colour / distortion heat / checker) and large UV-view redraws into a worker using OffscreenCanvas 2D. The worker returns a PNG Blob or an ImageBitmap. The drawing functions take an abstract 2D context and size, so the same code runs against a main-thread canvas when OffscreenCanvas is unavailable.

**Why:** The v4 plan draws exportUVPNG on the main thread. At 4096^2 with 100k faces, stroking about 300k segments and PNG-encoding blocks the UI for hundreds of ms (estimate). In a worker the UI stays interactive, and the UV view can use a background-rendered ImageBitmap while panning or zooming large meshes. A direct pixel rasteriser for per-face heat colours is also exact and faster than 100k separate fill() calls.

**Sketch:** Put the drawing code in a DOM-free factory (e.g. `UVRender.define('uvraster', function(C){...})`, reusing the same registry pattern) so its source can be appended to the worker Blob. Its functions are `drawWire(g, S, uv, faceEdgesOwner)`, `drawCharts(g, S, uv, faceChart)` and `rasterHeat(pixels /*Uint8ClampedArray S*S*4*/, S, uv, faceValues, lut)`. Worker op `renderUV({uv: Float32Array(6F) copy, faceChart, faceSD, size, style, bitmap: false})`: `const cv = new OffscreenCanvas(S, S); const g = cv.getContext('2d');` with y = (1-v)*S. Wire: draw each undirected UV edge once (skip the twin: draw only when the face index is the smaller one of the edge, or when the edge is a seam or boundary); batch into paths with `g.beginPath()` ... `lineTo` ... and `g.stroke()` every 20k segments; lineWidth = max(1, S/2048); 'butt' caps. Charts: hue h = (id * 0.618033988) % 1 converted to HSL(h*360, 55%, 60%); one path and one fill() per chart. Heat: a JS scanline rasteriser with the top-left rule writing a 256-entry turbo/viridis LUT colour (precomputed Uint8Array(768)) indexed by clamp((log2(SD) - lo)/(hi - lo)) into ImageData.data, then `g.putImageData(img, 0, 0)` and an optional wire overlay at 35% alpha. Export: `const blob = await cv.convertToBlob({type: 'image/png'}); postMessage({id, type: 'result', result: blob})` - a Blob is structured-cloned by reference, and its bytes are not copied through the JS heap. Previews: `const bmp = cv.transferToImageBitmap(); postMessage({id, type: 'result', result: bmp}, [bmp])`, then the main thread draws it with `ctx.drawImage(bmp, ...)` and calls `bmp.close()`. Fallback when `caps.offscreen2d` is false: run the same functions on `document.createElement('canvas')` and use `canvas.toBlob(cb, 'image/png')`. Size guard: cap S at 8192 (8192^2 RGBA = 256 MB); try smaller sizes if context creation returns null; warn on iOS where canvas memory limits are lower. Optionally combine this with the texel validator overlay for a 'bake preview' PNG.

**Risks:** Exact Safari version for OffscreenCanvas 2D in workers was not checked (Baseline March 2023 implies Safari 16.4). Canvas anti-aliasing differs slightly across browsers (not an issue for export). Very long single paths can be slow, hence the 20k-segment batching. uvraster must not reference DOM globals or it will break in the worker.

**Sources:** MDN OffscreenCanvas (available in Web Workers; 2d/webgl/webgl2/bitmaprenderer contexts; convertToBlob({type, quality}); transferToImageBitmap; Baseline widely available since March 2023) - https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/API/OffscreenCanvas; docs/ARCHITECTURE.md 5.x exporters.exportUVPNG({uv, faceChart, metrics, size=2048, style})

### F48. Crash-safe IndexedDB autosave + compact binary .uvtk project format (typed arrays + CompressionStream gzip) with runtime storage probing for file://

*Impact* high · *Effort* M · *Feasibility* high · **unverified** · *Target* `new src/io/storage.js (probe, IDB wrappers, autosave scheduler); src/io/exporters.js (saveProject/loadProject binary + legacy JSON); src/ui/main.js (restore toast, status)`

**What:** Automatic, deduplicated, typed-array-native persistence of the working session: mesh, manual seams, UVs, settings and a short undo history. On reload it offers 'Restore previous session'. A binary, gzip-compressed project file replaces base64 JSON. Storage support on file:// varies by browser and era, so a probe at startup selects IndexedDB, localStorage (settings only) or off, and shows the result in the UI.

**Why:** v3 and the v4 plan lose all work on a tab crash or reload. The planned project save is JSON with base64 arrays: +33% size over raw bytes and slow to parse, with no compression. Storing typed arrays directly in IDB avoids base64 and JSON entirely. Content-hash deduplication means autosave writes only UVs, cut and settings (~0.2 MB for the torus knot) instead of the whole mesh. A binary .uvtk file with gzip is typically several times smaller than base64 JSON (estimate) and loads zero-copy.

**Sketch:** Probe: `async function probeStorage(){ try { const db = await idbOpen('uvtk-probe', 1, d => d.createObjectStore('t')); await idbPut(db, 't', 'k', new Float32Array(4)); const back = await idbGet(db, 't', 'k'); db.close(); if (back instanceof Float32Array) return 'idb'; } catch (e) {} try { localStorage.setItem('uvtk-probe', '1'); localStorage.removeItem('uvtk-probe'); return 'ls'; } catch (e) { return 'none'; } }`, where idbOpen/idbPut/idbGet are ~30 lines of Promise wrappers. Status bar shows 'Autosave: on / settings only / off (serve over http to enable)'. Schema: DB 'uvtoolkit' v1 with stores 'meshes' (keyPath 'hash': {hash, name, faceCount, positions: Float32Array, byteLength, lastUsed}), 'sessions' (keyPath 'id': {id: 'autosave', meshHash, savedAt, appVersion, settings, manualCut: Uint8Array, uv: Float32Array, faceChart: Int32Array, history: [{uv, cut}] up to 10}) and 'handles' (keyPath 'name': {handle, lastOpened}, Chromium only). Hash: FNV-1a 32-bit over the Uint8Array view of positions (h ^= byte; h = Math.imul(h, 16777619) >>> 0, seed 2166136261), plus faceCount, giving a hex key; ~10 ms for 10 MB. Triggers: debounce 1500 ms after unwrap/relax/seam edit/settings change; flush on `visibilitychange` to hidden (the reliable last chance, since IDB transactions started in pagehide may not complete); coalesce so at most one transaction is in flight. Restore: at startup, if sessions.autosave exists and its meshHash is present, show a toast like 'Restore torus_knot (7,680 faces, 3 min ago)?'. Quota: `navigator.storage.estimate()`; when usage > 80% of quota evict LRU meshes not referenced by a session; call `navigator.storage.persist()` when served over https. The .uvtk binary: bytes 0-3 'UVTK', u32 version = 1, u32 headerByteLength, UTF-8 JSON header {name, appVersion, settings, arrays: [{key, dtype: 'f32'|'i32'|'u8', byteOffset, length}]} padded with spaces to a multiple of 8, then raw little-endian array data, each array 8-byte aligned so `new Float32Array(buf, off, len)` works without copying. Compress: `const gz = await new Response(new Blob([bytes]).stream().pipeThrough(new CompressionStream('gzip'))).blob()`. Load: detect gzip magic 0x1F 0x8B and decompress with DecompressionStream('gzip'); fall back to uncompressed when CompressionStream is undefined. Keep importing the v4 JSON project format. Serialization and compression can run in the worker, since IDB and CompressionStream are available in workers.

**Risks:** Current IndexedDB behaviour on file:// in Firefox and Safari is unverified (the available evidence is outdated or ambiguous), so the probe is mandatory and the UI must explain the fallback. Safari may evict script-writable storage for sites without recent user interaction. Schema changes need onupgradeneeded migrations. Autosave writes must never block the UI: structured clone of a large typed array happens synchronously in put(), so do it from the worker when possible.

**Sources:** MDN IndexedDB API - https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/API/IndexedDB_API; Mozilla bug 1005634 (IndexedDB origin handling for local files) - https://bugzilla.mozilla.org/show_bug.cgi?id=1005634; A. Powell, How the browsers store IndexedDB data (2012; historic report: Chrome allowed file:// IDB, Firefox did not - outdated) - https://www.aaron-powell.com/posts/2012-10-05-indexeddb-storage/; MDN CompressionStream - https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/API/CompressionStream; docs/ARCHITECTURE.md 5.x exporters.saveProject (JSON with base64 arrays)

### F49. File System Access open/save-in-place + drag-drop handles + file://-safe multi-file glTF import via an in-memory GLB packer

*Impact* medium · *Effort* M · *Feasibility* high · verified · *Target* `new src/io/files.js (pickers, handles, drag-drop, save-in-place), src/io/loaders.js (gltfToGlb packer, missing-resource report), src/ui/main.js (Ctrl+S / Recent menu)`

**What:** In Chromium: native open and save dialogs, a 'Save (Ctrl+S)' that overwrites the last exported file without prompting again, a 'Recent files' list backed by stored handles, and drag-drop that keeps a writable handle. In all browsers: a multi-file glTF import (.gltf + .bin, dropped or multi-selected) that concatenates the buffers into a single in-memory GLB and calls GLTFLoader.parse on it, so no URL fetch ever happens and it works identically on file:// and https.

**Why:** The v4 plan offers only download links and single-file inputs. A .gltf that references an external .bin cannot load from file://, because the loader fetches the relative URI and is blocked. The GLB packer fixes that everywhere. Save-in-place removes the download-folder round trip in the typical iterate-unwrap-export-bake loop. Handles stored in IDB give a working 'Recent files' menu.

**Sketch:** Detection: `const canPick = typeof window.showOpenFilePicker === 'function' && window.isSecureContext`. The pickers are Chromium-only; Firefox and Safari implement only the origin-private file system (navigator.storage.getDirectory), and the Chrome article's support table mixes the two. Fallback: `<input type=file multiple accept='.obj,.gltf,.glb,.bin,.stl,.ply,.uvtk'>` plus the existing downloadBlob. Open (inside a click handler): `const hs = await showOpenFilePicker({id: 'uvtk-models', multiple: true, types: [{description: '3D models', accept: {'model/obj': ['.obj'], 'model/gltf-binary': ['.glb'], 'model/gltf+json': ['.gltf'], 'model/stl': ['.stl'], 'application/octet-stream': ['.ply', '.bin']}}]}); const files = await Promise.all(hs.map(h => h.getFile()));` and store hs[0] in IDB 'handles'. Reopen from Recent: `if (await h.queryPermission({mode: 'read'}) !== 'granted' && await h.requestPermission({mode: 'read'}) !== 'granted') return;` (requestPermission needs user activation, so call it from the menu click). Save: `exportHandle = exportHandle || await showSaveFilePicker({id: 'uvtk-export', suggestedName: base + '_uv.glb', types: [{description: 'glTF binary', accept: {'model/gltf-binary': ['.glb']}}]}); if (await exportHandle.queryPermission({mode: 'readwrite'}) !== 'granted') await exportHandle.requestPermission({mode: 'readwrite'}); const w = await exportHandle.createWritable(); await w.write(blob); await w.close();` Ctrl+S reuses exportHandle, Ctrl+Shift+S clears it. Drag-drop: inside the drop event, synchronously map `e.dataTransfer.items` to promises (`item.getAsFileSystemHandle ? item.getAsFileSystemHandle() : Promise.resolve(null)`) and also call `item.getAsFile()`; await only after mapping, since the DataTransfer list is emptied once the event returns. Multi-file glTF: build `byName = new Map(files.map(f => [f.name, f]))`, parse the .gltf JSON, and for each buffers[i] take either a data: URI (base64-decode) or `byName.get(decodeURIComponent(uri.split('/').pop()))`, listing any missing names ('scene.bin missing - drop it together with the .gltf'). Concatenate into one BIN with 4-byte alignment, recording base[i]; for every bufferView set byteOffset += base[buffer] and buffer = 0; set json.buffers = [{byteLength: total}]. Drop external images/textures/samplers and remove material texture references (unwrapping needs geometry only); keep bufferView-embedded images if desired. Pack GLB: 12-byte header (u32 magic 0x46546C67, u32 version 2, u32 totalLength), JSON chunk (u32 length, u32 type 0x4E4F534A, UTF-8 padded with 0x20 to a multiple of 4), BIN chunk (u32 length, u32 type 0x004E4942, bytes padded with 0x00). Then `new THREE.GLTFLoader().parse(glb.buffer, '', onLoad, onError)`. Detect `extensionsRequired` containing KHR_draco_mesh_compression or EXT_meshopt_compression and show a clear 'compressed glTF not supported offline' message, because those decoders are not vendored.

**Risks:** Pickers exist only in Chromium desktop browsers. That file:// pages can use them follows from the secure-context rule but was not tested. Permissions do not persist across browser sessions, so re-prompts are expected. Stripping textures means the original texture cannot be previewed on the mesh (acceptable for a UV tool; could be restored later with blob URLs + LoadingManager.setURLModifier, but fetch of blob: URLs from a file:// origin is unverified).

**Sources:** Chrome for Developers: File System Access API (showOpenFilePicker/showSaveFilePicker/showDirectoryPicker, secure context + user gesture, handles storable in IndexedDB, queryPermission/requestPermission, createWritable, getAsFileSystemHandle, id/startIn/suggestedName) - https://developer.chrome.com/docs/capabilities/web-apis/file-system-access; MDN Secure Contexts (file: URLs are potentially trustworthy) - https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/Security/Secure_Contexts; Khronos glTF 2.0 specification, GLB binary layout - https://registry.khronos.org/glTF/specs/2.0/glTF-2.0.html#glb-file-format-specification

### F50. Three.js pinning under the classic-script constraint: r147 is the ceiling; hand-port single addons instead of moving to ES modules

*Impact* medium · *Effort* S · *Feasibility* high · verified · *Target* `vendor/three/* (+ VERSION file), src/io/loaders.js (compressed-glTF detection), tests/static-checks.test.js (no ES modules in index.html)`

**What:** A factual version strategy. examples/js (classic GLTFLoader, OrbitControls, exporters) was removed in r148, so r147 is the newest release with classic addons. build/three.min.js was deprecated in r150 and removed around r160/r161. ES modules are not an option because module scripts are fetched in CORS mode, which fails from a file:// origin. Stay on r141, or bump every vendored file atomically to r147. If a newer addon is needed, convert that single jsm file to a classic IIFE by hand.

**Why:** Prevents a tempting but breaking upgrade: any three.js >= r148 addon, WebGPURenderer or import maps would break file://, which is a hard requirement. It also gives a no-build path to individual newer loader fixes, and names the loaders that inherently need network fetches (Draco/KTX2 decoders, external textures) so their failures are handled deliberately instead of silently.

**Sketch:** (1) Keep vendor/three pinned. Add vendor/three/VERSION ('r141') and a startup assertion `if (THREE.REVISION !== '141') console.warn(...)` in the UI code (not in core). Any upgrade must replace three.min.js and all examples/js files from the same tag (r142-r147), followed by a manual smoke test: load OBJ/GLB/STL/PLY fixtures, export GLB, verify colours. (2) Hand-porting one addon from a newer jsm: wrap the file in `(function(){ ... })();`, replace `import { A, B } from 'three';` with `const { A, B } = THREE;`, replace `export { X };` with `THREE.X = X;`, and inline or strip any secondary imports (e.g. utils from BufferGeometryUtils). Keep a header comment 'converted from rNNN jsm, API-checked against core r141'. This only works when the addon uses core APIs that exist in r141, so check deprecated renames such as outputEncoding vs outputColorSpace (r152), which r141 does not have. (3) r141 specifics to rely on: `renderer.outputEncoding = THREE.sRGBEncoding` (pre-r152 colour management), `WebGLRenderTarget` with `samples` for MSAA, `renderer.capabilities.isWebGL2`, `GLTFExporter.parse(input, onDone, onError, {binary: true})` (check the vendored signature). (4) Network-dependent loaders are unsupported offline: DRACOLoader, KTX2Loader and external image textures fetch files. On file:// detect them up front (see the glTF packer finding) and show a message rather than a stuck spinner; when served over https they can be enabled later by vendoring the decoders. (5) Never add `<script type='module'>` or an import map; add a test that greps index.html for type='module' so no one reintroduces it.

**Risks:** Hand-ported addons can silently depend on newer core behaviour, so they must be tested. That module scripts fail from file:// is based on established Chromium/Firefox behaviour and was not re-verified in this session. r141 will not receive fixes; security exposure is low for a local tool.

**Sources:** three.js forum (maintainers): The examples/js directory will be removed with r148 - https://discourse.threejs.org/t/the-examples-js-directory-will-be-removed-with-r148/45349; three.js release r150 (deprecation warning on build/three.js and three.min.js) - https://github.com/mrdoob/three.js/releases/tag/r150; three.js forum: build/three.js deprecation (removed in r160/r161) - https://discourse.threejs.org/t/build-three-js-depreciation/53915

## Chart flattening and distortion optimisation beyond LSCM + SLIM + Progressive (BFF, cones, SCAF, untangling, projected Newton, ABF/LinABF, spectral conformal, multilevel, sparse direct solvers in JS)

### F51. Sparse LDLT direct solver (QDLDL-style up-looking) with geometric nested-dissection ordering, symbolic reuse and a prefactored-Laplacian PCG preconditioner

*Impact* high · *Effort* M · *Feasibility* high · verified · *Target* `src/core/solvers.js (ldltSymbolic, ldltNumeric, ldltSolve, orderND) - consumed by parameterize.js and optimize.js; benchmark page`

**What:** An exact sparse factorisation A = P^T L D L^T P for the symmetric positive (semi)definite systems in the pipeline. It has two phases. The symbolic phase computes the elimination tree and column counts in O(nnz(A)) and gives the exact nnz(L) before any numeric work. The numeric phase is up-looking. Both are preceded by a fill-reducing permutation. QDLDL is about 300 lines of C (etree, factor, Lsolve/Ltsolve/solve; returns -1 on an empty column or zero pivot), so a line-by-line JS port on Float64Array/Int32Array is easy. For 2-manifold chart graphs, geometric nested dissection (recursive median split on vertex coordinates, separator ordered last) gives O(n log n) fill and O(n^1.5) flops. Reverse Cuthill-McKee gives O(n^1.5) fill.

**Why:** The v4 plan solves every system with Jacobi/block2 PCG. Many of the pipeline's matrices never change: the LSCM normal matrix, the Tutte/harmonic Laplacian, the BFF Laplacian, the ARAP global matrix (cotan L x I2), and the spectral inverse iteration. For these, one factorisation followed by O(nnz(L)) back-solves is far cheaper. Rough JS estimates for a 50k-vertex chart with ND ordering: nnz(L) about 25-35 n (about 1.5M entries, 18 MB), factorisation 0.3-1 s, each solve 2-5 ms. Jacobi-PCG instead needs O(sqrt n), about 500-2000 SpMVs on a 350k-nnz matrix, which is 0.3-1 s per solve. LSCM with two distant pins is much worse conditioned and often stops short of 1e-8, and a partly solved LSCM/Tutte shows up as flips. ARAP iterations become back-solves only, about 10x faster. For SLIM (values change, pattern fixed) the exact nnz(L) from the symbolic phase lets the engine choose between numeric refactorisation and PCG. Blender recomputes a full SimplicialLDLT (symbolic analysis included) every iteration. Its initializer uses a general LU even though the matrix is SPD after eliminating the boundary.

**Sketch:** (1) orderND(adjStart, adjList, coords, dim, {boundaryLast}) -> perm Int32Array. Use an explicit stack of index ranges over one Int32Array. For a range of size <= 64, append the vertices in current order (optionally RCM inside). Otherwise take the axis with the largest bbox extent, quickselect the median, and split into Lset/Rset. The separator S is {v in Lset | some neighbour in Rset}. Push Lset minus S and Rset, and emit S after both subtrees (post-order). Use membership stamps (Int32Array tag = rangeId) to avoid Sets. With boundaryLast, first remove boundary vertices, run ND on the interior, and append the boundary. The leading |I|x|I| block of L is then exactly the factor of A_II, so one factorisation of the (grounded or regularised) full Laplacian serves Dirichlet solves (leading block only) and Neumann solves (full). BFF does the same with CAMD. For interleaved (u,v) systems expand as perm2[2k]=2perm[k], perm2[2k+1]=2perm[k]+1 so the 2x2 blocks stay adjacent. (2) symbolicLDLT(Acsr, perm): pinv = inverse perm. For each CSR entry (i,j,slot) with ni=pinv[i], nj=pinv[j], keep it when ni<=nj and place it in CSC column nj at row ni by counting sort. Keep slotMap Int32Array(nnzA) giving the CSC slot or -1, so later value refills are one loop over the SLIM/Newton per-triangle slot map. Etree: for k in 0..n-1, flag[k]=k, and for each row i<k in column k walk i -> parent[i] until flag==k, setting flag=k, Lnz[i]++, and parent[i]=k when it was -1. Lp = prefix sums of Lnz. Estimated flops = sum Lnz[k]^2. (3) numericLDLT: QDLDL_factor. For each column k: scatter column k (rows<k) into yVals and collect the reach via the etree (yMarkers, elimBuffer, yIdx) in topological order. For each reached i: yi = yVals[i]; for p in Lp[i]..LNext[i]-1, yVals[Li[p]] -= Lx[p]*yi; lki = yi*Dinv[i]; D[k] -= yi*lki; append (row k, lki) to column i; reset yVals[i]=0. Pivot guard: if D[k] < 1e-13*maxDiag, set D[k]=1e-13*maxDiag and count it as a regularised pivot, which is needed for the Neumann Laplacian with its constant null space. Alternatively ground the last vertex. (4) solveLDLT(F, b, x): permute, forward (L+I), divide by D, backward (L+I)^T, unpermute, with reused work buffers. (5) Engine policy: fixed matrices always use LDLT, cached per chart in a factor cache keyed by chart and matrix kind. For changing matrices (SLIM/PN), refactor when Lnz*12 bytes < 64 MB and estimated flops < about 3e8. Otherwise use PCG preconditioned by diag(Lw, Lw), where Lw is the scalar cotan Laplacian weighted per triangle by tr(W^T W)/2 plus the proximal term, refactored every 4 outer iterations with the symbolic phase reused. Expect about 5-30 PCG iterations instead of hundreds with block2. (6) Benchmark page: report symbolic nnz(L), factor ms, solve ms and PCG iterations on 2k/10k/50k hemisphere fixtures, and calibrate the thresholds from those numbers. Tests: relative residual < 1e-10 on a grid Laplacian; ND nnz(L) < natural-order nnz(L); leading-block Dirichlet solve equals a separately factored A_II solve.

**Risks:** The JS timings are estimates from flop counts and have not been measured. Geometric ND on charts that fold strongly in 3D gives worse separators, so use the current UV/projection coordinates when available, which is still correct and only affects fill. Memory for 2n interleaved factors of 50k-vertex charts (about 6M entries, 70 MB) is too much in a worker, so the size-based fallback to PCG is essential. The Apache-2.0 attribution must be kept if QDLDL is ported literally.

**Sources:** QDLDL (OSQP project, Apache-2.0) source: https://raw.githubusercontent.com/osqp/qdldl/master/src/qdldl.c (read); Blender 4.3 SLIM uses Eigen SimplicialLDLT recomputed every solve, proximal_p = 0.0001: https://raw.githubusercontent.com/blender/blender/main/intern/slim/intern/slim.cpp (read); Blender SLIM initializer uses Eigen SparseLU: https://raw.githubusercontent.com/blender/blender/main/intern/slim/intern/uv_initializer.cpp (read); BFF timings with CHOLMOD, about 10 ms to factor and about 1 ms per solve at 10k triangles, plus the CAMD block-ordering trick: https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/1704.06873 (read); T. Davis, Direct Methods for Sparse Linear Systems (CSparse), SIAM 2006; George 1973 nested dissection; Lipton-Rose-Tarjan 1979 (background knowledge, not fetched)

### F52. Boundary First Flattening (BFF): free-boundary minimal-area-distortion conformal map, plus exact boundary control (disk, rectangle, prescribed corner angles, painted boundary scale)

*Impact* high · *Effort* M · *Feasibility* high · verified · *Target* `src/core/parameterize.js (solveBFF, bffBoundaryFromScale, bffBoundaryFromAngles, bffToRectangle, bffToDisk); UV-view tool 'Straighten island' and 'Map to disk'; preset option`

**What:** A linear conformal flattening. It solves the discrete Yamabe/Cherrier problem for the log conformal scale factor u with the cotan Laplacian A (A_ij = -1/2(cot b_p + cot b_q), A_ii = -sum_j A_ij). Interior angle defects are Omega_i = 2pi - sum of corner angles; boundary geodesic curvatures are k_i = pi - sum of corner angles. It converts between boundary scale u_B and boundary curvature k~ with Dirichlet-to-Neumann / Neumann-to-Dirichlet (Poincare-Steklov) maps, integrates the target boundary curve, closes it with a 2x2 least-squares length correction, and extends it to the interior by a harmonic function a and its discrete conjugate b. Setting u_B = 0 gives the minimal-area-distortion conformal map. The total cost is one Cholesky factorisation of A and three back-solves.

**Why:** It replaces LSCM's two-pin solution and the circle-boundary Tutte init with a pin-free, discretisation-stable conformal map whose boundary is chosen for low area distortion. Tutte maps the boundary to a circle and needs Progressive Parameterisation to recover, so BFF gives a much lower starting energy for SLIM, fewer iterations, and usually no need for the progressive outer loop. Blender's SLIM init maps the boundary to a unit circle with uniform angular spacing. Speed from the paper: about 10 ms to factor and about 1 ms per solve for 10k triangles in C++, around 50x faster than CETM. Expect roughly 5-10x that in JS with the LDLT finding. BFF also enables new user-facing tools that neither v3 nor v4 has. 'Straighten to rectangle' prescribes k~ = pi/2 at four corners and 0 elsewhere. Closure changes only lengths, never directions, so the island edges come out exactly axis-aligned, which suits trim sheets and packs at nearly 100%. 'Map to disk' and 'set corner angles' use the same machinery, and painted boundary scale is interactive because only back-solves re-run.

**Sketch:** Precompute: corner angles from 3D (clamp to [1e-8, pi-1e-8]); A from cotangents plus 1e-8*meanDiag on the diagonal; Omega_I; k_B. Order interior first and boundary last (orderND with boundaryLast) and factor once. Define DtN(phi, g): a_I = A_II^-1 (phi_I - A_IB g), then h = phi_B - A_BI a_I - A_BB g. Define NtD(phi, h): solve the full A u = phi - [0; h], subtract the mean, return u_B. Sign convention (derive-and-test): use phi = [-Omega_I ; 0], h = DtN(phi, u_B) and k~ = k - h. Then sum(k~) = sum(k) + sum(Omega_I) = 2pi exactly by Gauss-Bonnet; assert |sum k~ - 2pi| < 1e-8 as a unit test. DEFAULT u_B = 0. Boundary curve: walk boundaryLoops[0] in triangle-winding (CCW) order, vertices p = 0..m-1, with edge p going from p to p+1 and 3D length l_p. Tangent angle phi_p = sum_{i<=p} k~_i and T_p = (cos phi_p, sin phi_p). Target length l*_p = exp((u_p+u_{p+1})/2) l_p. Closure (paper eq. 19-20 with N_ii = 1/l): M = sum_p l_p T_p T_p^T (2x2), r = sum_p l*_p T_p, lambda = M^-1 r, l~_p = l*_p - l_p (T_p . lambda). Then gamma_0 = 0 and gamma_{p+1} = gamma_p + l~_p T_p; the residual adjustments are typically about 1e-3 relative. Interior extension (conformal mode): a_I = A_II^-1(-A_IB Re gamma) using the leading-block solve. Hilbert-transform Neumann data h_j = 1/2(a_k - a_i) for consecutive boundary vertices i -> j -> k. Solve the full Neumann system A b = [0 ; h] and set uv = (a, b). If the total signed UV area < 0, negate b (orientation depends on loop direction). Shape mode (rectangle/disk/fixed angles) uses dual harmonic extension instead: b_I = A_II^-1(-A_IB Im gamma), which reproduces the boundary polygon exactly. Rectangle: auto-pick 4 corners as the boundary vertices with the largest k_i, at least perimeter/8 apart in arc length, or let the user click them. Set k~ = pi/2 there and 0 elsewhere, u_B = NtD(phi, k - k~), then lengths, closure, dual-harmonic extension. Disk: about 10 fixed-point iterations of d_i = 1/2(l~_{i-1} + l~_i), k~_i <- 2pi d_i / sum d, k~ <- 1/2(k~_new + k~_old), each followed by NtD, lengths and closure; finish with dual-harmonic extension. Painted boundary scale: u_B = user values, then DtN. Finally normalizeChartScale. Register as initChart method 'bff', the new default. Pipeline: bff -> countFlips -> if > 0 then untangle (see that finding) or Tutte fallback -> SLIM/PN. Flips are rare; the paper reports about 1% of models with about 1e-5 relative area affected.

**Risks:** Local injectivity is not guaranteed. Theory needs convex target angles and Delaunay-ish meshes, and flips are rare but possible, so keep the flip check and fallback. Conjugate-harmonic output only approximately matches the target boundary, so use dual-harmonic extension for exact shapes. Negative cotan weights on obtuse meshes are fine for the solve but reduce injectivity guarantees. Charts must be disks (already guaranteed by cutToDisk). Sign and orientation conventions must be fixed by unit tests: planar patch gives k~ = k and a similarity map; hemisphere satisfies Gauss-Bonnet.

**Sources:** Sawhney & Crane, Boundary First Flattening, ACM TOG 2018 (arXiv 2017): https://arxiv.org/abs/1704.06873 and https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/1704.06873 (read); Reference implementation: https://github.com/GeometryCollective/boundary-first-flattening

### F53. BFF cone singularities: greedy cone placement (max |u|), cut through cones, and a scale-factor heatmap as a seam-suggestion tool

*Impact* medium · *Effort* M · *Feasibility* medium · verified · *Target* `new src/core/cones.js (placeCones, coneCurvatures, cutThroughCones) used by chart.js cutToDisk and parameterize.js solveBFF; UI seam-suggestion overlay`

**What:** Area distortion of a conformal flattening is e^{2u}. Letting a few interior vertices (cones) absorb curvature (u = 0 there) and cutting from each cone to the boundary removes most of that distortion with short seams. Greedy placement: solve the Dirichlet problem with u = 0 on the boundary and existing cones, pick the vertex with the largest |u|, and repeat. The cone angle defect is whatever curvature that vertex ends up concentrating. BFF then flattens the cut disk with the Dirichlet data u restricted to the new boundary.

**Why:** In the v4 plan, a closed or strongly curved chart is either bisected by cutToDisk (two charts, a long seam, more packing waste) or left to SLIM, which fights large area distortion. With cones a sphere-like or bulging chart stays one island with a few short cut paths, and area distortion is bounded by a threshold the user chooses. The |u| heatmap computed before cutting doubles as a 'seam suggestion' overlay for the manual seam tool. Neither v3 nor the v4 plan predicts distortion before flattening.

**Sketch:** Input: disk chart local (plus mesh edges for cutting) and the BFF factor. (1) u0 = A_II^-1(-Omega_I) with Dirichlet 0 on the boundary. (2) Greedy loop for up to maxCones (UI slider 0-32; 'auto' stops when max|u| < 0.3466 = ln(2)/2, i.e. local area scale within 2x). Choose c = argmax_{i in I \ C} |u_i|, excluding vertices within 2 rings of an existing cone. Add the Dirichlet constraint without refactoring, using a capacitance update: g_c = A_II^-1 e_c (one back-solve per cone, stored); S = G[C,C] (k x k dense); u = u0 - G_C S^-1 u0[C]. (3) Cone curvature: with the final u, Theta_c = Omega_c + (A u)_c at cones and k~_B = k_B + (A u)_B at the boundary. Gauss-Bonnet holds automatically because A's rows sum to 0; normalise as BFF does (scale so the sum is 2*pi*chi) against round-off. Optional 'seamless' mode rounds Theta_c to multiples of pi/2 and puts the rounding residual on the boundary. (4) Cut: process cones by decreasing |Theta|. Run multi-source Dijkstra on 3D edge lengths using chart.js shortestVertexPath with targets = boundary vertices plus already-cut vertices, and mark the path edges in the cut Uint8Array. For closed genus-0 pieces (loops === 0), follow ConePlacement.cpp: the first cone is the max-curvature vertex (chi > 0), cones are connected by a Dijkstra tree, and the result feeds cutToDisk instead of bisection. (5) Rebuild with buildChartLocal (vertex splitting duplicates cut vertices, and both copies keep the same u) and run BFF with g = u on the new boundary: k~ = k - DtN(phi, g), lengths l* = exp((u_i+u_j)/2) l, closure, conjugate extension. For seamless output, give paired cut edges a single shared length unknown in the closure step (paper). (6) UI: a 'Cones' slider, cone glyphs in the 3D view, an e^{2u} heatmap (log2 scale, diverging colours) and a 'suggest seams' button that writes the Dijkstra paths into the manual seam layer. Cost: one factorisation plus (maxCones + 3) back-solves plus Dijkstra.

**Risks:** Genus > 0 still needs homology cuts (tree-cotree or the existing bisection); cones alone do not make a disk. Cone paths add seam length, so the UI should show the trade-off. Greedy max|u| is a heuristic, not an optimal placement. Seamless quantisation can raise distortion. Up to a few cones the capacitance update is cheap, but beyond about 50 cones refactoring is simpler. The exact BFF normalisation code was only partially read.
